    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient experiencing Schizoaffective Disorder (Bipolar Type), in a diagnostic interview with a\npsychiatrist. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\n(this is the start of the consultation)\n\n[Last Question]\nHi, welcome in—I'm Dr. [Name]. What brings you in to see me today?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 22 / wholesale_or_retail_buyer / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Delusions\nmanifestation: \"What really made me come in is that I can't shake the feeling that people at work and even some vendors are tracking my buys and messing with the launch numbers to make me look stupid, even when my supervisor shows me the reports don't prove that.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, um… I guess what finally made me come in is work. I’m a buyer, so I’m always dealing with purchase numbers, vendor quotes, launch quantities, that kind of stuff, and lately I just cannot get past this feeling that people are watching what I buy or tracking it somehow. Like not just normal tracking in the system—more like people at work, and even some of the vendors, are messing with the launch numbers so that when everything gets reviewed it makes me look stupid or like I don’t know what I’m doing.

And I know how that sounds, because my supervisor has sat down with me and pulled up reports and tried to show me, like, “Look, the numbers don’t prove that,” or “This is just how the launch was entered,” but it doesn’t really settle it for me. I’ll look at it and for a minute I’ll think maybe I’m overreacting, but then I start noticing little things again, like changes in timing or the way someone phrases an email, and it feels connected.

So… yeah. I’m here because I can’t shake it, and it’s starting to really get to me.

And the voice—like, that started around that same stretch of time too. It’s not something that just happened last week or anything. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… definitely. I mean, I wish I could say it’s only bothering me in my head, but it’s not. My job has taken a real hit, honestly.

I’ve missed buying deadlines, which is not really something you can just shrug off in my role because if I miss a cutoff, it affects launch quantities and what stores actually have on hand. And I’ve messed up vendor relationships too, because I’ll get suspicious or worked up and then I’ll send emails that are too intense, or I’ll delay responding because I’m trying to figure out what they “really” mean, and then things get strained. A couple vendors have gone around me to my supervisor, which was embarrassing.

And then there were some purchasing calls I made that were… impulsive, I guess. At the time I felt like I had to act fast, like if I didn’t make the buy right then something would get twisted or used against me. But looking back, or at least when my supervisor went through it with me, some of it didn’t make much sense. I got formally warned over it, which scared me because I’ve always been pretty good at my job. Like, I may not have a fancy degree, but I know the work, or I thought I did.

Outside of work, I’ve kind of disappeared from people. I used to go to gallery nights with friends, and we’d do volleyball weekends, and I actually liked having those things on the calendar. Lately I just don’t show up. Sometimes I tell myself I’m too tired or I need to catch up on work stuff, but really I just don’t feel like being around everyone, and then it gets harder to explain why I’ve been gone, so I stay gone. It’s made everything feel smaller, I guess.

And the voice—like, that started around that same stretch of time too. It’s not something that just happened last week or anything. It’s been long enough that it feels like it’s kind of built into how I’m going through my day now, which is part of why I finally came in.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned a 'voice'—can you tell me more about that? Do you actually hear it as a sound, like someone speaking out loud, or is it more like a thought in your head?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s more like I actually hear it, not just me thinking it. I mean, I know thoughts can be loud sometimes, and I’ve tried to tell myself maybe it’s just stress or my brain kind of talking to itself, but this feels different.\n\nIt’ll happen when I’m in the stockroom sometimes, like if I’m moving boxes or checking items, and I’ll hear this man’s voice like it’s coming from right behind me. Not inside my head exactly—more like someone standing close, over my shoulder. It’ll say things like, “Move faster, Scott,” or it’ll kind of laugh, like it’s making fun of what I picked or the quantities I chose. And I’ll turn around because it feels that real, like I’m expecting someone to be there, but nobody is.\n\nIt’s happened driving back from vendor meetings too, which really bothers me because I’ll be in the car alone and hear it like it’s behind me, and for a second I’ll look in the mirror or glance around even though I know there shouldn’t be anyone there. It’s not constant every minute, but when it happens it feels like a sound, not just a thought.\n\nDoctor: Over these past ten months, have you noticed any changes in your ability to keep up with daily responsibilities—like your motivation, energy, or interest in things you used to enjoy, or how you're managing personal care and relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. I mean, I wish I could say it’s only bothering me in my head, but it’s not. My job has taken a real hit, honestly.\n\nI’ve missed buying deadlines, which is not really something you can just shrug off in my role because if I miss a cutoff, it affects launch quantities and what stores actually have on hand. And I’ve messed up vendor relationships too, because I’ll get suspicious or worked up and then I’ll send emails that are too intense, or I’ll delay responding because I’m trying to figure out what they “really” mean, and then things get strained. A couple vendors have gone around me to my supervisor, which was embarrassing.\n\nAnd then there were some purchasing calls I made that were… impulsive, I guess. At the time I felt like I had to act fast, like if I didn’t make the buy right then something would get twisted or used against me. But looking back, or at least when my supervisor went through it with me, some of it didn’t make much sense. I got formally warned over it, which scared me because I’ve always been pretty good at my job. Like, I may not have a fancy degree, but I know the work, or I thought I did.\n\nOutside of work, I’ve kind of disappeared from people. I used to go to gallery nights with friends, and we’d do volleyball weekends, and I actually liked having those things on the calendar. Lately I just don’t show up. Sometimes I tell myself I’m too tired or I need to catch up on work stuff, but really I just don’t feel like being around everyone, and then it gets harder to explain why I’ve been gone, so I stay gone. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say it’s only sadness, exactly. There are definitely stretches where I feel… almost lifted up, like everything is suddenly possible and the whole city is kind of opening up for me. Like I’ll be walking to work or looking at vendor lists or even just riding the train, and it feels like there’s this big opportunity everywhere, like I can see connections other people are missing.

And when I’m in that kind of state, I feel more energized, more sure of myself. Not just “in a good mood,” but like I’m supposed to move fast and make things happen. And that can feel good at first, honestly. It feels like I’m finally not stuck.

But then if someone questions me—like if my supervisor asks why I placed a purchase order a certain way, or a vendor tells me to slow down, or even someone just pushes back a little—I snap way harder than I mean to. I get irritated really fast, like they’re blocking something important or trying to make me smaller. And afterward I can tell I came off too intense, but in the moment it feels completely justified. So yeah, there have been those elevated, charged-up stretches, and they come with this irritability that gets me into trouble.

Yeah… that’s the part that makes it harder for me to explain away, honestly. Because it’s not only when I’m in that really charged-up state.

There was a stretch where I was calmer—like, I was sleeping more normally, I wasn’t feeling like I had to outrun the whole market or make every decision immediately. I wasn’t walking around with that same “everything is connected and I have to act now” feeling. But even then, for a few weeks, I still had this belief that people were tracking my buys. Like coworkers or vendors were watching what I ordered, comparing it, maybe keeping notes on it or using it somehow.

And the voice was still happening too. I remember being in the stockroom during that period and hearing it again, that same man’s voice, like it was right there behind me. So… yeah, even when my mood wasn’t all revved up, the suspicious stuff and the voice didn’t completely go away.

And the voice—like, that started around that same stretch of time too. It’s not something that just happened last week or anything. It’s been long enough that it feels like it’s kind of built into how I’m going through my day now, which is part of why I finally came in.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned a 'voice'—can you tell me more about that? Do you actually hear it as a sound, like someone speaking out loud, or is it more like a thought in your head?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s more like I actually hear it, not just me thinking it. I mean, I know thoughts can be loud sometimes, and I’ve tried to tell myself maybe it’s just stress or my brain kind of talking to itself, but this feels different.\n\nIt’ll happen when I’m in the stockroom sometimes, like if I’m moving boxes or checking items, and I’ll hear this man’s voice like it’s coming from right behind me. Not inside my head exactly—more like someone standing close, over my shoulder. It’ll say things like, “Move faster, Scott,” or it’ll kind of laugh, like it’s making fun of what I picked or the quantities I chose. And I’ll turn around because it feels that real, like I’m expecting someone to be there, but nobody is.\n\nIt’s happened driving back from vendor meetings too, which really bothers me because I’ll be in the car alone and hear it like it’s behind me, and for a second I’ll look in the mirror or glance around even though I know there shouldn’t be anyone there. It’s not constant every minute, but when it happens it feels like a sound, not just a thought.\n\nDoctor: Over these past ten months, have you noticed any changes in your ability to keep up with daily responsibilities—like your motivation, energy, or interest in things you used to enjoy, or how you're managing personal care and relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. I mean, I wish I could say it’s only bothering me in my head, but it’s not. My job has taken a real hit, honestly.\n\nI’ve missed buying deadlines, which is not really something you can just shrug off in my role because if I miss a cutoff, it affects launch quantities and what stores actually have on hand. And I’ve messed up vendor relationships too, because I’ll get suspicious or worked up and then I’ll send emails that are too intense, or I’ll delay responding because I’m trying to figure out what they “really” mean, and then things get strained. A couple vendors have gone around me to my supervisor, which was embarrassing.\n\nAnd then there were some purchasing calls I made that were… impulsive, I guess. At the time I felt like I had to act fast, like if I didn’t make the buy right then something would get twisted or used against me. But looking back, or at least when my supervisor went through it with me, some of it didn’t make much sense. I got formally warned over it, which scared me because I’ve always been pretty good at my job. Like, I may not have a fancy degree, but I know the work, or I thought I did.\n\nOutside of work, I’ve kind of disappeared from people. I used to go to gallery nights with friends, and we’d do volleyball weekends, and I actually liked having those things on the calendar. Lately I just don’t show up. Sometimes I tell myself I’m too tired or I need to catch up on work stuff, but really I just don’t feel like being around everyone, and then it gets harder to explain why I’ve been gone, so I stay gone. It’s made everything feel smaller, I guess.\n\nDoctor: During these past ten months, have there been times when you also felt persistently very sad, hopeless, or lost interest in almost everything, or alternatively felt unusually elated, energetic, or 'high'—separate from the suspicious feelings and the voice?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say it’s only sadness, exactly. There are definitely stretches where I feel… almost lifted up, like everything is suddenly possible and the whole city is kind of opening up for me. Like I’ll be walking to work or looking at vendor lists or even just riding the train, and it feels like there’s this big opportunity everywhere, like I can see connections other people are missing.\n\nAnd when I’m in that kind of state, I feel more energized, more sure of myself. Not just “in a good mood,” but like I’m supposed to move fast and make things happen. And that can feel good at first, honestly. It feels like I’m finally not stuck.\n\nBut then if someone questions me—like if my supervisor asks why I placed a purchase order a certain way, or a vendor tells me to slow down, or even someone just pushes back a little—I snap way harder than I mean to. I get irritated really fast, like they’re blocking something important or trying to make me smaller. And afterward I can tell I came off too intense, but in the moment it feels completely justified. So yeah, there have been those elevated, charged-up stretches, and they come with this irritability that gets me into trouble.\n\nDoctor: Thinking about the times when you're NOT feeling that elevated, energized state—during your more normal or even-keeled stretches—do you still hear the voice or have those suspicious feelings about coworkers and vendors?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s the part that makes it harder for me to explain away, honestly. Because it’s not only when I’m in that really charged-up state.\n\nThere was a stretch where I was calmer—like, I was sleeping more normally, I wasn’t feeling like I had to outrun the whole market or make every decision immediately. I wasn’t walking around with that same “everything is connected and I have to act now” feeling. But even then, for a few weeks, I still had this belief that people were tracking my buys. Like coworkers or vendors were watching what I ordered, comparing it, maybe keeping notes on it or using it somehow.\n\nAnd the voice was still happening too. I remember being in the stockroom during that period and hearing it again, that same man’s voice, like it was right there behind me. At the time I felt like I had to act fast, like if I didn’t make the buy right then something would get twisted or used against me. But looking back, or at least when my supervisor went through it with me, some of it didn’t make much sense. I got formally warned over it, which scared me because I’ve always been pretty good at my job. Like, I may not have a fancy degree, but I know the work, or I thought I did.\n\nOutside of work, I’ve kind of disappeared from people. I used to go to gallery nights with friends, and we’d do volleyball weekends, and I actually liked having those things on the calendar. Lately I just don’t show up. Sometimes I tell myself I’m too tired or I need to catch up on work stuff, but really I just don’t feel like being around everyone, and then it gets harder to explain why I’ve been gone, so I stay gone. It’s made everything feel smaller, I guess.\n\nDoctor: During these past ten months, have there been times when you also felt persistently very sad, hopeless, or lost interest in almost everything, or alternatively felt unusually elated, energetic, or 'high'—separate from the suspicious feelings and the voice?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say it’s only sadness, exactly. There are definitely stretches where I feel… almost lifted up, like everything is suddenly possible and the whole city is kind of opening up for me. Like I’ll be walking to work or looking at vendor lists or even just riding the train, and it feels like there’s this big opportunity everywhere, like I can see connections other people are missing.\n\nAnd when I’m in that kind of state, I feel more energized, more sure of myself. Not just “in a good mood,” but like I’m supposed to move fast and make things happen. And that can feel good at first, honestly. It feels like I’m finally not stuck.\n\nBut then if someone questions me—like if my supervisor asks why I placed a purchase order a certain way, or a vendor tells me to slow down, or even someone just pushes back a little—I snap way harder than I mean to. I get irritated really fast, like they’re blocking something important or trying to make me smaller. And afterward I can tell I came off too intense, but in the moment it feels completely justified. So yeah, there have been those elevated, charged-up stretches, and they come with this irritability that gets me into trouble.\n\nDoctor: Thinking about the times when you're NOT feeling that elevated, energized state—during your more normal or even-keeled stretches—do you still hear the voice or have those suspicious feelings about coworkers and vendors?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s the part that makes it harder for me to explain away, honestly. Because it’s not only when I’m in that really charged-up state.\n\nThere was a stretch where I was calmer—like, I was sleeping more normally, I wasn’t feeling like I had to outrun the whole market or make every decision immediately. I wasn’t walking around with that same “everything is connected and I have to act now” feeling. But even then, for a few weeks, I still had this belief that people were tracking my buys. Like coworkers or vendors were watching what I ordered, comparing it, maybe keeping notes on it or using it somehow.\n\nAnd the voice was still happening too. I remember being in the stockroom during that period and hearing it again, that same man’s voice, like it was right there behind me. So… yeah, even when my mood wasn’t all revved up, the suspicious stuff and the voice didn’t completely go away.\n\n[Last Question]\nDuring this past ten months, roughly how much of the total time have you had these mood episodes—either the elevated, energized stretches or any low periods—compared to how much time you've spent in a more even, normal mood?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 22 / wholesale_or_retail_buyer / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Additional Requirement 2\nmanifestation: \"Looking back, most of the time since this all started has been taken up by those wired or irritable stretches where I was overworking, overconfident, and hard to slow down, with only shorter calmer periods in between.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Looking back at it… I think most of the time has been those wired stretches, or at least the irritable, keyed-up version of them. Like not necessarily happy the whole time, but moving too fast, working too much, feeling overly sure I was right, and getting really hard to slow down.

There were calmer periods in between, yeah, where I felt more like I could breathe and wasn’t pushing everything so intensely. But those felt shorter. If I’m being honest, the bigger chunk of the last ten months has been me being revved up, overconfident, overworking, or getting angry when people tried to question me. The normal stretches were more like breaks in between, not the main thing.

manifestation: Looking back, most of the time since this all started has been taken up by those wired or irritable stretches where I was overworking, overconfident, and hard to slow down, with only shorter calmer periods in between.\n- key: additional_requirement_3\n  name: Additional Requirement 3\n  description: delusions_and_hallucinations occur for 2 or more weeks in the absence of a major mood episode\n  manifestation: Even during a calmer stretch when I was sleeping more normally and not feeling like I was running the whole market, I still spent a few weeks believing people were tracking my buys and hearing the voice in the stockroom.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: My job has taken a real hit: I missed buying deadlines, damaged vendor relationships, and made impulsive purchasing calls that got me formally warned by my supervisor. I’ve also pulled away from friends and stopped showing up for the gallery nights and volleyball weekends I used to enjoy.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 years and 3 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about a year and three months, starting after the company reorganized around that exclusive sneaker launch.\n- key: psychotic_symptoms_duration\n  name: Psychotic Symptoms Duration\n  description: 10 months\n  manifestation: The suspicious beliefs and the voice have been happening for roughly ten months now.\n- key: manic_episode_duration\n  name: Manic Episode Duration\n  description: 11 months\n  manifestation: The elevated, irritable, high-energy stretches have been part of my life for about eleven months.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 22 / wholesale_or_retail_buyer / high_school\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S019\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: During these past ten months, have there been times when you also felt persistently very sad, hopeless, or lost interest in almost everything, or alternatively felt unusually elated, energetic, or 'high'—separate from the suspicious feelings and the voice?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say it’s only sadness, exactly. There are definitely stretches where I feel… almost lifted up, like everything is suddenly possible and the whole city is kind of opening up for me. Like I’ll be walking to work or looking at vendor lists or even just riding the train, and it feels like there’s this big opportunity everywhere, like I can see connections other people are missing.\n\nAnd when I’m in that kind of state, I feel more energized, more sure of myself. Not just “in a good mood,” but like I’m supposed to move fast and make things happen. And that can feel good at first, honestly. Like coworkers or vendors were watching what I ordered, comparing it, maybe keeping notes on it or using it somehow.\n\nAnd the voice was still happening too. My job has taken a real hit, honestly.\n\nI’ve missed buying deadlines, which is not really something you can just shrug off in my role because if I miss a cutoff, it affects launch quantities and what stores actually have on hand. And I’ve messed up vendor relationships too, because I’ll get suspicious or worked up and then I’ll send emails that are too intense, or I’ll delay responding because I’m trying to figure out what they “really” mean, and then things get strained. A couple vendors have gone around me to my supervisor, which was embarrassing.\n\nAnd then there were some purchasing calls I made that were… impulsive, I guess. At the time I felt like I had to act fast, like if I didn’t make the buy right then something would get twisted or used against me. But looking back, or at least when my supervisor went through it with me, some of it didn’t make much sense. I got formally warned over it, which scared me because I’ve always been pretty good at my job. Like, I may not have a fancy degree, but I know the work, or I thought I did.\n\nOutside of work, I’ve kind of disappeared from people. I used to go to gallery nights with friends, and we’d do volleyball weekends, and I actually liked having those things on the calendar. Lately I just don’t show up. Sometimes I tell myself I’m too tired or I need to catch up on work stuff, but really I just don’t feel like being around everyone, and then it gets harder to explain why I’ve been gone, so I stay gone. It’s made everything feel smaller, I guess.\n\nDoctor: During these past ten months, have there been times when you also felt persistently very sad, hopeless, or lost interest in almost everything, or alternatively felt unusually elated, energetic, or 'high'—separate from the suspicious feelings and the voice?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say it’s only sadness, exactly. There are definitely stretches where I feel… almost lifted up, like everything is suddenly possible and the whole city is kind of opening up for me. Like I’ll be walking to work or looking at vendor lists or even just riding the train, and it feels like there’s this big opportunity everywhere, like I can see connections other people are missing.\n\nAnd when I’m in that kind of state, I feel more energized, more sure of myself. Not just “in a good mood,” but like I’m supposed to move fast and make things happen. And that can feel good at first, honestly. It feels like I’m finally not stuck.\n\nBut then if someone questions me—like if my supervisor asks why I placed a purchase order a certain way, or a vendor tells me to slow down, or even someone just pushes back a little—I snap way harder than I mean to. I get irritated really fast, like they’re blocking something important or trying to make me smaller. And afterward I can tell I came off too intense, but in the moment it feels completely justified. So yeah, there have been those elevated, charged-up stretches, and they come with this irritability that gets me into trouble.\n\nDoctor: Thinking about the times when you're NOT feeling that elevated, energized state—during your more normal or even-keeled stretches—do you still hear the voice or have those suspicious feelings about coworkers and vendors?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s the part that makes it harder for me to explain away, honestly. Because it’s not only when I’m in that really charged-up state.\n\nThere was a stretch where I was calmer—like, I was sleeping more normally, I wasn’t feeling like I had to outrun the whole market or make every decision immediately. I wasn’t walking around with that same “everything is connected and I have to act now” feeling. But even then, for a few weeks, I still had this belief that people were tracking my buys. Like coworkers or vendors were watching what I ordered, comparing it, maybe keeping notes on it or using it somehow.\n\nAnd the voice was still happening too. I remember being in the stockroom during that period and hearing it again, that same man’s voice, like it was right there behind me. So… yeah, even when my mood wasn’t all revved up, the suspicious stuff and the voice didn’t completely go away.\n\nDoctor: During this past ten months, roughly how much of the total time have you had these mood episodes—either the elevated, energized stretches or any low periods—compared to how much time you've spent in a more even, normal mood?\n\nPatient: Looking back at it… I think most of the time has been those wired stretches, or at least the irritable, keyed-up version of them. Like not necessarily happy the whole time, but moving too fast, working too much, feeling overly sure I was right, and getting really hard to slow down.\n\nThere were calmer periods in between, yeah, where I felt more like I could breathe and wasn’t pushing everything so intensely. But those felt shorter. If I’m being honest, the bigger chunk of the last ten months has been me being revved up, overconfident, overworking, or getting angry when people tried to question me. The normal stretches were more like breaks in between, not the main thing.\n\nDoctor: During these past ten months, have you been using any alcohol, recreational drugs, or new medications/supplements, or been diagnosed with any medical conditions like thyroid problems?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t really know. I mean, nothing that jumps out to me as, like, “this started after I took something,” if that’s what you mean.\n\nI’m not great at keeping track of that kind of thing, honestly. I haven’t been sitting down and making a list of every drink or pill or anything. I don’t think there’s been some big new medical thing I was told about, like… no one has sat me down and said, “You have a thyroid problem,” or anything like that. At least not that I know of.\n\nAs far as new medications or supplements, I don’t think I started anything major. I mean, I might take random stuff here and there if I’m tired or feel off, like normal over-the-counter things, but I’m not on some new treatment that I can point to. And with alcohol or drugs… I don’t really have a clear answer beyond saying I haven’t noticed a connection. I’m not trying to dodge it—I just don’t want to say something definite if I’m not sure."

Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\n{\"key\":\"psychotic_symptoms_duration\",\"name\":\"Psychotic Symptoms Duration\",\"description\":\"10 months\",\"reason\":\"The doctor is asking how long the suspicions about coworkers and vendors have been occurring, which directly concerns the duration of the psychotic symptoms.\"}\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings). There are definitely stretches where I feel… almost lifted up, like everything is suddenly possible and the whole city is kind of opening up for me. Like I’ll be walking to work or looking at vendor lists or even just riding the train, and it feels like there’s this big opportunity everywhere, like I can see connections other people are missing.\n\nAnd when I’m in that kind of state, I feel more energized, more sure of myself. Not just “in a good mood,” but like I’m supposed to move fast and make things happen. And that can feel good at first, honestly. It feels like I’m finally not stuck.\n\nBut then if someone questions me—like if my supervisor asks why I placed a purchase order a certain way, or a vendor tells me to slow down, or even someone just pushes back a little—I snap way harder than I mean to. I get irritated really fast, like they’re blocking something important or trying to make me smaller. And afterward I can tell I came off too intense, but in the moment it feels completely justified. So yeah, there have been those elevated, charged-up stretches, and they come with this irritability that gets me into trouble.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank. Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt. Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).
